=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two positive integers n and k.
An integer x is called k-palindromic if:

x is a palindrome.
x is divisible by k.

Return the largest integer having n digits (as a string) that is k-palindromic.
Note that the integer must not have leading zeros.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 3, k = 5
Output: "595"
Explanation:
595 is the largest k-palindromic integer with 3 digits.

Example 2:

Input: n = 1, k = 4
Output: "8"
Explanation:
4 and 8 are the only k-palindromic integers with 1 digit.

Example 3:

Input: n = 5, k = 6
Output: "89898"

 
Constraints:

1 <= n <= 10^5
1 <= k <= 9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def largestPalindrome(self, n: int, k: int) -> str:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def largestPalindrome(self, n: int, k: int) -> str:
        if k == 1:
            return '9' * n
        # precompute powers mod k up to n-1
        pow10 = [1] * n
        for i in range(1, n):
            pow10[i] = (pow10[i-1] * 10) % k
        m = (n + 1) // 2
        # possible[i] bitset of remainders achievable from position i..m-1
        possible = [0] * (m + 1)
        possible[m] = 1  # bit 0
        # We need maybe compute weights for each i,d? Could precompute weight_mod[i][d]? m*10 =5e5, okay. But not necessary.
        for i in range(m-1, -1, -1):
            start = 1 if i == 0 else 0
            bits = 0
            # compute base weight without d? w = d * factor mod k.
            if n % 2 == 1 and i == m-1:
                factor = pow10[n//2] % k
            else:
                factor = (pow10[n-1-i] + pow10[i]) % k
            # For each d, w = (d * factor) % k
            # shift possible[i+1] by w
            nxt = possible[i+1]
            for d in range(start, 10):
                w = (d * factor) % k
                # shift bits in nxt by w modulo k
                shifted = 0
                # Could do:
                # for r in range(k):
                #    if nxt & (1<<r): shifted |= 1 << ((r+w)%k)
                # But can optimize with bit operations:
                # low = (nxt << w) & mask; high = nxt >> (k-w) if w>0; shifted = low | high
                # Because modulo k bitset rotation left by w.
                if w == 0:
                    shifted = nxt
                else:
                    mask = (1 << k) - 1
                    shifted = ((nxt << w) & mask) | (nxt >> (k - w))
                bits |= shifted
            possible[i] = bits
        # Greedy
        rem = 0
        half = []
        for i in range(m):
            start = 1 if i == 0 else 0
            if n % 2 == 1 and i == m-1:
                factor = pow10[n//2] % k
            else:
                factor = (pow10[n-1-i] + pow10[i]) % k
            nxt = possible[i+1]
            for d in range(9, start-1, -1):
                w = (d * factor) % k
                target = (rem + w) % k
                if nxt & (1 << target):
                    half.append(str(d))
                    rem = target
                    break
            else:
                # Should not happen
                pass
        # Build palindrome
        if n % 2 == 0:
            return ''.join(half) + ''.join(reversed(half))
        else:
            return ''.join(half) + ''.join(reversed(half[:-1]))

Need check bitset rotation formula. possible[i+1] has bits for remainder r. Adding w gives r+w mod k. If bitset has bit r set. Left shift by w moves bit r to r+w. Bits with r+w >= k wrap to r+w-k. Formula:
mask = (1<<k)-1
shifted = ((nxt << w) & mask) | (nxt >> (k-w)) for w>0.
Example k=5, nxt bit0=1, w=3. nxt<<3 = bit3, &mask bit3; nxt >> (2) =0 -> bit3. Good. bit r=4,w=3 -> r+w=7 mod5=2. nxt bit4. nxt<<3 has bit7 not in mask (k=5 mask bits0..4) so dropped; nxt>>(2) has bit (4-2)=2. Good. For w=0, nxt>>k? avoid. Good.

But note possible[i+1] bitset may include bits beyond k? No, mask. Fine.

Greedy target 0. Fine. But mask=(1<<1)-1=1. Could handle. But early return okay. Need ensure for k=1, n up to 1e5, return '9'*n. Since largest palindrome divisible by1 is all 9s. Good.

So possible[i] = union over d of rotate(possible[i+1], w). This is O(10) per i. Very fast. We don't need loop r. Good.

Let's verify rotation formula for bitset with multiple bits:
Suppose k=5, nxt bits {0,4}=10001, w=3. Rotated {3,2} (4+3=7 mod5=2). Formula:
nxt<<3 = bit3 + bit7. mask bits0..4 => bit3. nxt>>(2) = bit (4-2)=2. OR => bits2,3. Good.

Need define mask = (1 << k) - 1. For k=9, mask511. Good.

Before final, let's thoroughly verify the DP and greedy with possible bitset rotation.

Detailed proof:
Let m = ceil(n/2). For each half position i, define w_i(d) = d * f_i mod k, where f_i = (10^{n-1-i}+10^i) mod k for pair, f_i = 10^{n//2} mod k for center. Then value mod k = Σ w_i(d_i). We need choose d_i with d_0∈1..9, others∈0..9, sum ≡0.

Define S_i = set of remainders achievable from positions i..m-1. S_m={0}. S_i = ∪_{d∈D_i} { (w_i(d)+r) mod k | r∈S_{i+1} }. We compute bitset. Then greedy: At position i, with current remainder R from previous positions, choose largest d such that (R+w_i(d)) mod k ∈ S_{i+1}. This ensures completion. Since we choose largest at each position, lexicographically maximum. At end R=0. Good.

Let's maybe write code:

class Solution:
    def largestPalindrome(self, n: int, k: int) -> str:
        if k == 1:
            return "9" * n

        pow10 = [1] * n
        for i in range(1, n):
            pow10[i] = (pow10[i-1] * 10) % k

        m = (n + 1) // 2
        possible = [0] * (m + 1)
        possible[m] = 1
        mask = (1 << k) - 1

        for i in range(m - 1, -1, -1):
            if n % 2 == 1 and i == m - 1:
                factor = pow10[n // 2]
            else:
                factor = (pow10[n - 1 - i] + pow10[i]) % k

            start = 1 if i == 0 else 0
            nxt = possible[i + 1]
            bits = 0
            for d in range(start, 10):
                w = (d * factor) % k
                shifted = ((nxt << w) & mask) | (nxt >> (k - w))
                bits |= shifted
            possible[i] = bits

        rem = 0
        half = []
        for i in range(m):
            if n % 2 == 1 and i == m - 1:
                factor = pow10[n // 2]
            else:
                factor = (pow10[n - 1 - i] + pow10[i]) % k

            start = 1 if i == 0 else 0
            nxt = possible[i + 1]
            for d in range(9, start - 1, -1):
                w = (d * factor) % k
                target = (rem + w) % k
                if nxt & (1 << target):
                    half.append(str(d))
                    rem = target
                    break

        if n % 2 == 0:
            return ''.join(half) + ''.join(reversed(half))
        else:
            return ''.join(half) + ''.join(reversed(half[:-1]))

Need ensure `factor` is int mod k. For pair, pow10 values already mod k; sum may be >=k, mod. For center, pow10 already mod k. Good.

However, if k=1, `n // 2` etc. no issue. Early return all 9s is correct. But note all 9s is palindrome and divisible by1. Good.

```

    ...
```

Option 2:
```
possible = [0]*(m+1)
possible[m]=1
for i in range(m-1, 0, -1):
    start = 0
    ...
    possible[i] = bits
```
Then possible[0] remains 0. Greedy for i=0 uses possible[1]. This avoids computing possible[0] and the `start = 1 if i == 0 else 0` in DP. But in greedy, for i=0, start=1. This is slightly more efficient and clearer? It might be less intuitive because possible[0] not computed. But possible[0] is not needed. However, if m=1, loop range(0,0,-1) empty; possible[1]=1. Greedy i=0 uses possible[1]. Good.

Which is less error-prone? Computing all possible including possible[0] aligns with recurrence and ensures possible[0] exists but unused. It also uses start=1 in DP, but that's correct. I'll keep computing all possible; no harm. The code is simple.
